The speakers "the Samādhi of the Buddhas" and "the Great Bodhisattva" are rendered literally as epithets, as the Tibetan gives them. In the vajra-family samādhi verse, "drawing you forth from desire" follows the Tibetan as it stands; the addressee is not specified.  The "secret accomplishment" verses speak of killing, in the idiom of the tantric rites of forceful liberation; the translation keeps the Tibetan wording. In several of these verses it is unclear whether the qualities listed (for example "without wrath," "do not tame beings") describe the targets of the rite or failings of the practitioner; they are read here as describing the targets, in parallel with the other verses. "The Fulfiller of All Hopes" (re ba thams cad skong ba) is rendered literally; it may stand for Sarvāśāparipūraka.  "Never content with the families and the tantras" renders an ambiguous line; རྒྱུད here could also mean "lineage," giving "not satisfied with family and lineage." "This sūtra in which the Great Vehicle is gathered" renders ཐེག་པ་ཆེན་པོ་མངོན་འདུས་པ, which may reflect Mahāyānābhisamaya, an alternative title of this tantra. The speaker "the Hūṃ of All Tathāgatas" reads ཐམས་ཅད་ཀྱིས as ཐམས་ཅད་ཀྱི. The names པདྨའི་འདོད་པ and ནོར་བུ་བཞེད་པ could not be identified with confidence and are translated literally as "Lotus Desire" and "Jewel Desire." The section-closing རྫོགས་སྷོ is read as རྫོགས་སོ, "is complete."  མི་འགྲུངས in the vajra-family Dharma verse is obscure; it is rendered "unfit." The names དམ་ཚིག་དམ་པ and རྡོ་རྗེ་དགྱེས་པ could not be identified with confidence and are translated as "the Supreme Commitment" and "Vajra Delight." In the karma verse of the Tathāgata, བུད་མེད་ལུགས is taken as a cast image of a woman; in the next verse སེམས་མེད་པ is taken as "one with no inclination." The couplet "This is the consort of the All-Pervading; through giving all, I and the rest" is terse and its construal is uncertain.  The first verse of the mudrā section ends with ནའོ and reads as an instruction on when the address is used; its construal is somewhat uncertain. The names རྡོ་རྗེ་འགྱིང་བའི་བདག་པོ and བཀའ་བཞིན་དུ་མཛད་པ could not be identified with confidence and are translated as "the Lord of Vajra Grace" and "He Who Acts on the Blessed One's Command." In "the mudrās of all the families," the Tibetan has རིག་པ ("knowledge"), read here as རིགས ("family") to match the parallel section closings. ཕྱག་བཞི་པོ is taken as "the four mudrās"; it might also mean "the four-armed one." རབ་དགའ is taken here as the state of great joy, not a name.  རྡོ་རྗེ་འཇོ་མ, here "the vajra charm," seems to correspond to the first of the four inner offering goddesses (Vajralāsyā), followed by garland, song and dance; the Tibetan word is unusual. The name པདྨའི་སྤྱན is given as Padmanetra, on the pattern of Vajranetra. The verse on the offerings of song and dance has no closing attribution in the Tibetan.  In the lotus-family Dharma commitment verse, སྒོ་མ is read as སྒོམ, "meditating," as in the parallel verses. དོན་གྲུབ་པ is taken as a short form of the name Sarvārthasiddhi and given as Arthasiddhi. The speaker ནོར་བུའི་ཆོས could not be identified with confidence and is translated as "the Jewel Dharma." The section-closing རྫོགས་སྷོ is read as རྫོགས་སོ.  The attribution of the first verse reads literally "the Blessed One, the noble Mañjuśrī, all the tathāgatas"; it is taken here as identifying Mañjuśrī with all the tathāgatas. The second verse under the tathāgata family in the section on the action of absorption, and the second in the section on subtle wisdom, are both headed "tathāgata family" in the Tibetan, as translated. In the vajra-family verse on the action of absorption, the first line (sdig pa dag pa rgyu ba'i phyir) is obscure and is rendered "so that sin may be purified." The name padma bzhad pa is rendered literally as "the Blossoming Lotus," since its Sanskrit form cannot be identified with confidence. "Rites" renders rtog pa in the sense of ritual manual (kalpa).  In the lotus-family verse on the eye, ser ba is the color yellow in a list of colors, not hail. In the verse on the wall drawing, the words rendered "a likeness" (ldan 'dra ba) and "raised the sign" (mtshan bsgreng) are obscure; the sense of the rite, bringing a woman under one's power, is clear, but the exact procedure is uncertain. The speaker names rdo rje dbang rin po che ("the Precious Vajra Empowerment") and padma chags pa ("the Lotus of Passion") and the form 'gro 'dul ("the Tamer of Beings") are rendered by their meaning, since their Sanskrit forms cannot be fixed with confidence. The attribution of the final verse follows in the next piece.  The verse after the opening of the ninth fascicle reads ltа ba ngan cing chags bral, "of wrong views and devoid of passion"; in this tantric context passion is part of the path, so those without it are judged unfit, but the reading is somewhat unexpected. "Ritual systems" renders brtag pa (kalpa).  "A hundred times" renders lan brgya rtsa, which may mean "a full hundred" or be short for a hundred and eight. The "rite of descent" (dbab pa) is the rite of causing a deity or power to descend upon persons or objects. In the list of emblems, "hand" and "row of teeth" correspond to the gestures of approval and of laughter in the series of sixteen great bodhisattvas. "To punish" renders chad pas gcad pa, not the philosophical sense of annihilation.  "Turning the wheel of time" renders dus kyi 'khor los sgyur ba, here one of a list of magical powers, not the Kālacakra system. The Blessed One "Tamer of All Beings" ('gro ba thams cad 'dul ba) is a form of Avalokiteśvara; the name is rendered by its meaning. In the jewel-family section the text gives don thams cad grub pa, rendered Sarvārthasiddhi as the central deity of that family.  "Ancillary practice" renders nye bar sbyong ba, apparently a subsidiary practice (upasādhana) of the jewel family; the exact Sanskrit equivalent is uncertain. The text says magical power is of four kinds but then lists many attainments; it is translated as it stands. In the vajra vidyādhara section, the instrumental rdo rje rig 'dzin gyis ("by the vajra vidyādhara") is read as rig 'dzin gyi, "a wheel-turning sovereign of the vajra vidyādharas," in line with the parallel phrase in the preceding passage.  "Padma Kāma" renders padma 'dod pa, which may be a deity name or simply "the lotus of desire"; it is kept as a name in line with the names already used. "The Sattva Without Beginning or End" translates thog ma dang tha ma med pa'i sems dpa', an epithet whose Sanskrit form could not be confidently identified. The opening phrase "for the sake of the heart of one's own mudrā" is awkward in the Tibetan and is rendered literally.  The final section plays on the Tibetan rtog pa, which renders both "rite" (Skt. kalpa) and "conception" (Skt. kalpa, vikalpa), and in the first verse also on rtag pa, "permanent"; the English cannot keep the pun, so "rite" is used in the headings and "conception" in the verses. The opening line reads kyi yi for kyi, an evident scribal slip. "Supreme Wisdom" translates shes rab mchog and "Vajramati" renders rdo rje blo; neither epithet could be identified with full confidence.  In the verse on the vidyā, the last line reads rtog pa, "rite" (also playing on rtogs, "realizing"), where the pattern of the preceding verses would lead one to expect rig pa, "vidyā"; it is translated as written. "The eightfold practice" renders brgyad bsgrub, whose exact sense here (perhaps a stance or posture) is uncertain. "Vajravāk" renders rdo rje gsung and "Padmasattva" renders padma'i sems dpa'.  "The Realization of the Great Vehicle" renders theg pa chen po mngon 'dus pa, which appears to reflect Mahāyānābhisamaya, an alternative title of this tantra. In the hymn, "Host of sentient beings" renders sems can tshogs, which is terse in the Tibetan; "Teacher" reads ston pa for ston pa pa po, an evident duplication of a syllable.  The lines "Because the steadfast, as those to be trained require, abide even in the views of non-Buddhists" render a terse Tibetan couplet whose sense is not fully certain; it may also mean that the steadfast cause beings to abide in such views for the sake of training them. The god addressed by Maheśvara, rendered "the Mighty One," translates a Tibetan epithet whose Sanskrit original (possibly Mahābala or Nārāyaṇa) could not be identified with confidence. Maheśvara's closing speech expresses the worldly gods' mistaken view, which the text goes on to refute.  The seat the gods ask the Buddha to leave is called a "ran" seat in the Tibetan; since the text elsewhere calls it the seat of grass, it is rendered so here, though the word itself is unusual. In the sixth stanza "great brilliance" renders an obscure term (lham pa chen po) whose exact sense is uncertain. The name in the first line of the sixteenth stanza, literally "Vajra Bound," is translated rather than given a Sanskrit form, as its Sanskrit original is not certain from the Tibetan. The closing title phrase may also be read as "the sūtra and its abridged tantra." The final syllable of the closing title is written "sho," an evident slip for the usual "so" ending, and is read as such.

The following is a note about this translation.

This is an AI-generated translation from the AI Stupa project. It was produced by a careful, doctrine-aware process aiming at a high degree of accuracy, but it has not been reviewed by a human translator and may contain errors. It is offered as a provisional translation for those texts of the Tibetan Buddhist canon that have not yet received an authoritative human translation. Where a published translation exists — above all from 84000: Translating the Words of the Buddha (84000.co) — that translation is definitive and should be preferred; please consult it instead. These translations are released into the public domain (CC0) in the spirit of spreading the Dharma to all corners of the earth.

Just as the Bodhi tree now standing at Bodh Gayā neither is nor is not the same tree beneath which the Buddha attained awakening, so too this stupa is intended as the next generation of stupa — dependently arising according to the conditions of its environment, and ceaselessly proclaiming the Dharma for the benefit of all sentient beings.

May whoever encounters these words derive great benefit.
